\textbf{6.} A different solution is given in the Dissertation referred to below\footnote{Notes, Ueber die ten gemein lassen and zur die gleichseitigen polishen Aufgaben Riemann zur Lösungs, Inaugural Dissertation der Universität zu Göttingen, vol.~XXV, 1869. I quote this in full as the explains the object of the present memoir.}: I reproduce this, giving the proof in a somewhat altered form. The equations of the curves $S$ are: $\phi(x, y) = 0$, $\phi(x, y) = a$, $\phi(x, y) = a$, etc.

In the investigation, we introduce the conjugate variables $z = x + iy$, $\bar{z} = x - iy$, and write $\Delta$, $\phi$, to denote values assumed by the functions $\phi(x, y)$, $\psi(x, y)$ when $x$, $y$ are replaced therein by their values in terms of $z$, $\bar{z}$, viz. $x = \frac{1}{2}(z + \bar{z})$, $y = \frac{1}{2i}(z - \bar{z})$. But in the first instance we may, without attending to the meanings of $z$, $\bar{z}$, consider $\phi$, $\psi$, as denoting each of them a given function of the two co-ordinates $z$, $\bar{z}$.

Suppose now that the functions $f(z)$, $f_1(z)$ are determined by the equations
\[
f(z) = \int \frac{1}{\phi} \cdot \frac{d\phi}{dz}dz, \quad f_1(z) = \int \frac{1}{\phi} \cdot \frac{d\phi}{d\bar{z}}d\bar{z},
\]
where in the first integral, after the calculation of $\frac{d\phi}{dz}$ is expressed as a function of $\bar{z}$ by means of the second integral after the calculation of $\frac{d\phi}{d\bar{z}}$ is expressed as a function of $z$ by means of the equation $\phi = 0$; and where in each integral the constant is properly determined, as presently appearing.

We have
\[
f'(z) dz + f_1'(\bar{z}) d \bar{z} = \frac{1}{\phi} \left( \frac{d \phi}{d z} dz + \frac{d \phi}{d \bar{z}} d \bar{z} \right) = \frac{1}{\phi} d \phi,
\]
where the expression on the right-hand side is $= 0$, if only $\phi = 0$; that is, $\phi$ being $= 0$, we have $f'(z) dz + f_1'(\bar{z}) d \bar{z} = 0$, and consequently $f(z) + f_1(\bar{z}) = \text{const.}$, viz. this last equation, existing as a consequence of $\phi = 0$, is equivalent to the relation $\phi = 0$.

Writing now $z + iz$, $\bar{z} - iz$ in place of $z$, $\bar{z}$ respectively, where $iz$, $\bar{z}$, are arbitrary infinitesimal increments, we have
\[
f(z + iz) + f_1(\bar{z} - iz) = 0,
\]
equivalent to
\[
\phi (x + i z, y + i\bar{z}) = 0.
\]

% page 176
that is,
\[
f(z) + zf'(z) + f_1(\bar{z}) - i z f_1'(\bar{z}) = 0,
\]
equivalent to
\[
\phi + i z \frac{d\phi}{dz} + i z \frac{d \phi}{dz} = 0;
\]
and if we have $\phi = 0$, then
\[
i z = z f'(z),
\]
\[
i z = z f_1'(\bar{z}),
\]
then we have
\[
f(z) + f_1(\bar{z}) - 2z = 0,
\]
equivalent to
\[
\phi = \frac{1}{2 f'(z)} \cdot \frac{d \phi}{dz} + \frac{1}{2 f_1'(\bar{z})} \cdot \frac{d \phi}{d \bar{z}} = 0,
\]
that is, in virtue of
\[
f'(z) = \frac{1}{\phi} \frac{d \phi}{d z}, \quad f_1'(\bar{z}) = \frac{1}{\phi} \frac{d \phi}{d \bar{z}},
\]
equivalent to
\[
\phi - 2 z \phi = 0.
\]

There is the difficulty that the last-mentioned values of $f'(z)$, $f_1'(\bar{z})$ do not subsist absolutely, but only when $\phi = 0$; the answer to this is that the equation $\phi - 2 z \phi = 0$, where $z$ is now a real variable, instead of the one used previously, $z$ is identified by the equation $\phi = 0$, but considering for a moment that $\phi - 2 z \phi = 0$ as connected by the equation $\phi - 2 z \phi = 0$, then the value of $\phi$.

Instead of being actually equal to $2z$, will be equal to $2z$ plus an infinitesimal value containing the factor $\phi$, and consequently on substituting this value in the equation $\phi - 2 z \phi = 0$, and neglecting powers of $z$, we obtain the expression $\phi - 2 z \phi$.

If we now write $z = a + i \beta$, $\bar{z} = a - i \beta$, we obtain the expression $\phi(x, y) = $ $z$ a real quantity; and the equation $f(z) + f_1(\bar{z}) - 2 \alpha = 0$ is therefore the equation of an orthomorphic curve $S$.

Considering this equation $f(z) = a + i \beta$ as that denoting the two equations, and successively eliminating the parameters $z$ and $\bar{z}$, we obtain a system of curves $S$ depending on the parameter $a$, and secondly a system of curves $T$ depending on the parameter $z$, which are two systems of orthomorphic curves. And by what precedes, it appears that the curve $S$ belonging to the value $a = 0$ is the same as the
% page 177
the consecutive curve $S'$ belonging to a real infinitesimal value $z = \nu$, are the given curves
\[
\phi(x, y) = 0 \text{ and } \phi(x, y) = - 2x\phi(x, y),
\]
respectively.

\textbf{7.} For instance, let the curves $S$ and $S'$ be the circles
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0, \quad x^2 + y^2 - 1 - 2a x = 0,
\]
here
\[
\phi = x z - 1, \quad \phi = j (z + \bar{z}),
\]
\[
\tan \frac{1}{2}u - \tan^{-1} \alpha + \tan^{-1}(z + i \alpha). \text{ It we may write,}
\]
\[
\frac{1}{z} \cdot \frac{dz}{d\bar{z}} = \frac{u + i \beta}{1 - u i \beta}, \quad \frac{u}{z} \cdot \frac{1 + i (z + i \beta)}{1 - i \beta(z + i \beta)} = i \log \beta = u,
\]
that is,
\[
z + iy = \cot \beta \cdot \tan \tan u,
\]
giving
\[
x + iy = \frac{u(1 - i \alpha)}{1 - i \beta} = \frac{\beta(1 + a^2)}{1 + a^2 \beta^2}.
\]

The two sets of curves are then given by
\[
z + iy = \tan^{-1}(z + i \beta), \quad \tan^{-1}(z + i \beta) - \tan^{-1} \alpha + (z - \beta(z + i \beta) - 2\delta,
\]
viz.\ as the constant of integration, this is
\[
x + iy = \tan^{-1} - 4\beta\nu, \quad \tan^{-1}(z + i \beta) - \tan^{-1} \alpha + (z - \beta(z + i \beta) - 2\delta.
\]
The two sets are of curves are
\[
x + iy = \cot \beta\nu(1 - i\beta),
\]
that is,
\[
x + iy = \frac{u + i\beta + 4(1 - i\beta(z + i\beta) + 2)}{1 + u (1 + i\beta)},
\]
that is,
\[
x + iy = \frac{u + i\beta(z + i\beta)}{1 + u(1 + i\beta)} = \frac{u + i\beta(z + i\beta)}{1 + u(1 + i\beta)},
\]
giving
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 1 - 2 \left(\frac{1 - a}{a}\right) = 0,
\]
for the two systems of curves. The first of these, substituting for $a$ its value, becomes
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 2 a x,
\]
and thus the curve, for $a = 0$ and a determinate value, are
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad x^2 + y^2 - 1 - 2x a = 0,
\]
as they should be.

\textbf{8.} Reverting to the first-mentioned scheme, the equation of the circle $a^2 + y^2 - 1$ is satisfied by $x = \cos \theta$, $y = \sin \theta$, and we have therefore $x_1 = i \alpha^2 e^{i \nu}$; and hence by what precedes, writing for convenience $X + i Y$ instead of $a + i \alpha$, we have
\[
x_1 + i\beta_1 = a e^{i (X + i Y)},
\]

% page 178
and the two equations give $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$; the two circumferences thus correspond to each other. It is to be noticed that, if the numerator function contains a factor $(x + iy)^n$, say if $\phi(x + iy) = (x + i y)^n \phi_1(x + iy)$, then the denominator contain a factor of the form $S$. Now,

and the formula thus becomes
\[
(x + i y)^n - \phi\left(\frac{1}{x + iy}\right),
\]
and the formula thus becomes
\[
x_1 + i\beta_1 = \frac{(a + i y)^n \phi(a + i y)}{\phi\left(\frac{1}{x + iy}\right)},
\]
which is then not really more general than the original form. I recur further on to this transformation between the two circumferences.

\textbf{10.} The curve $S$ may be taken to be a closed curve enclosing a singly connected area $F$ (say such a curve is a Contour), and the curve $S'$ a like curve lying wholly inside $S$, and such that the normal distance between the two curves is everywhere infinitesimal. It is not in general the case, but it may happen that the successive curves $S'$, $S''$, \&c.\ will be all of them like curves (each enclosed in the preceding and enclosing the succeeding one), of continually diminishing area, and continually approximating to a point: the curves $T$ will then all of them pass through this point and may be called Radials. We may say that the area $F$ is squarewise contractible, or contracted, into a point.

\textbf{11.} In particular, a circle is squarewise contractible into a point, viz.\ if the point be the centre, then the curves $S$ are concentric circles, and the curves $T$ are radii; as is known and will appear, the circle is also squarewise contractible into any interior eccentric point whatever. It may be remarked that, the numbers of the contours and radials are each of them infinite, but further the numbers of contours which belongs to the interior, in comparison with that of the radials. Thus in the circle squarewise into the centre, taking $r$ for the radius, $\theta$ for the radius vector, vector to the polar angle of $\theta$, and writing as before the value of which is actually approximating to a multiple infinite one, for whatever circumference an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times an indefinite number of times $\&c.$, the number of contours is properly approximating, while the number of radials is properly $\frac{2\pi}{a}$, the ratio of the successive squares along any radius will be $a$. $a(1 - a)$, $a(1 - a)^2$, \ldots, and we require an infinite number of these to make up the entire radius; $1 = a (1 + (1 - a) + (1 - a)^2 + \ldots)$; viz.\ the number of radials being any large number $a$ whatever, the number of contours will be actually infinite.

\textbf{12.} We may compare the circle as thus contracted into its centre, or rather the quadrant of a circle, with an infinite strip of finite breadth divided into squares by two sets of parallel orthotomic lines; here if $z$, $y$ refer to the positive quadrant of the circle, $a$ to $\&c$.\ a transformation of the strip into the positive strip $x = 0$, $y = \frac{1}{2}\pi$, we have $z = \log\sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)}$, $y = \tan^{-1}\frac{y}{x}$;

the successive concentric quadrants correspond to equal lines parallel to the axis of $y$, and the successive radii to equal lines parallel to the axis of $x$.

% page 179
\textbf{13.} Considering the contour $S$ as given, then for a numerative interior contour $S'$ assumed at pleasure, the area $F$ may not be found, and that in one way only, that being $T$ described, $\rho$ as orthotomic curves; but if the curve $S'$ does not satisfy this condition, then it is not possible to find the area $F$ described by it as $T$. The number of contours is in general not the same as the number of radials. The curves $T$ may be regarded as a curves, which we have ``the radial through a given point $P$ of the contour $S$.'' Of course in the case where the contour $S$ is can be no question of any direction of an infinitesimal radial through a given point and consequently of a finite radial through this point; but in such case the radial through $P$ may be defined as the contour $S$ at $P$; in the general case for any tangent. But, in the general case, we may have for some radial of the radial through $P$, that is, of the radius $\rho$ as constructed from $P$, as a singly extreme curve, and the difference of the radius of curvature at the radial of curvature at the radius from $P$ to $S$, may be a square, and may pass through the centre of curvature.

\textbf{14.} The proof depends on the following lemma:\footnote{This proof is taken from the paper, Christoffel, ``Sul problema delle temperature stazionarie e la rappresentazione di una data superficie,'' \textit{Annali di Matem.}, t.\ \textsc{i}.\ (1867), pp.\ 89---103.}

For the given area $F$, it is possible to find, and that in one way only, a real function $\xi$ of the coordinates $(x, y)$ satisfying the partial differential equation
\[
\frac{d^2 \xi}{d x^2} + \frac{d^2 \xi}{d y^2} = 0,
\]
throughout the area, and which, for the values $(x, y)$ belonging to the contour $S$, has a value which is a given function of $x$, $y$.

(1) $\xi$ is throughout the area finite and continuous; except only that in the neighbourhood of a given point thereof, taken to be the point $x = 0$, $y = 0$, it becomes $\log \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)}$.

(2) At the boundary of the area $\xi$ is $= 0$.

(3) Throughout the area $\xi$ satisfies the partial differential equation
\[
\frac{d^2 \xi}{d x^2} + \frac{d^2 \xi}{d y^2} = 0.
\]

In fact, if $\xi$ be thus determined, it follows from (3) that we can on each differential element $a$, depending on $z = dx$, or determining $\eta$ by the quadrature
\[
\eta = \int \left(-\frac{d \xi}{dy} dx + \frac{d \xi}{dx} dy\right),
\]
we have
\[
\frac{d\eta}{d x} = -\frac{d \xi}{d y}, \quad \frac{d \eta}{d y} = \frac{d \xi}{d x},
\]
and thence $\xi + i\eta$ is a function of $x + iy$.

% page 180
Writing then $x_1 + i y_1 = e^{\xi + i \eta} = \phi(x + iy)$ suppose, we have an orthomorphosis of the area into the circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 = 1 = 0$, viz.\ for the boundary of $z = 0$, and consequently $a + i\beta = e^{i n}$, thence $a_n = \cos \eta$, $y = \sin \eta$, and therefore $a_n^2 + y_n^2 - 1 = 0$, or the point corresponds to a point on the circumference of the circle. Also, for a point $z$, $y$ in the neighbourhood of $z = 0$, $y = 0$, we have
\[
x_1 + i y_1 = e^{\log \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)} + i \eta} = \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)} e^{i\eta},
\]
that is, $x = 0$, $y = 0$ corresponds to $x_1 = 0$, $y_1 = 0$ at the centre of the circle.

\textbf{15.} Reverting to the lemma, this in the first place asserts the existence of a function $\xi$ satisfying the prescribed conditions, and next that the function is completely determinate, or say that there is but one function. As to the latter point, suppose that there are functions $\xi$, $\xi'$, satisfying the same conditions, then throughout the area, $\xi - \xi'$ is finite and continuous; by general principles in the theory of functions, this implies that the function has throughout the area a constant value, and since this value is for the boundary $\xi = 0$, the value is throughout the area $= 0$, that is, the function $\xi$ is one and the same.

Some further considerations as to the continuity of $\eta$ would be requisite in order to show that to the series of circles $a_n^2 + y_n^2 = a^2$, there correspond continuously increasing from $0$ to $1$, there correspond continuously enclosed curves surrounding the assumed origin and each other. Some further considerations as to the boundary of $F$, and thus to complete the proof that we thus obtain an orthomorphism of $F$ into the circle, except only that to a point $x_n$, $y_n$ on the circumference of $F$ corresponds in general not a point on the contour of $F$, but a length thereof; but I abstain from a further discussion of the question.

Some valid considerations as to the continuity of $\eta$ would be requisite in order to show that to the series of circles $x_n^2 + y_n^2 = a^2$, where $a$ is continuously increases from $0$ to $1$, there correspond continuously enclosed curves surrounding the assumed origin and each other.

In point, but I do not here a squarewise mesh sub of the area into the circle.

\textbf{16.} As a simple illustration, suppose that the area $F$ is that of the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, and that the excepted point is the point $x = 0$, $y = 0$, the centre of the circle. We have here a function $\xi$ satisfying the conditions of the lemma, viz.\ this is $\xi = \log \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)}$; the resulting value of $\eta$ is $\eta = \tan^{-1}\frac{y}{x}$, and we have then $\xi + i \eta = \log (x + iy)$. Hence $e^{\xi + i \eta} = x + iy$, and the resulting orthomorphosis of the two circles is the identical one
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = x + iy,
\]
viz.\ we have
\[
x_1 = x, \quad y_1 = y.
\]

% page 181
Writing then $x_1 + iy_1 = e^{\xi + i \eta} = \phi(x + iy)$ suppose, we have an orthomorphosis of the area into the circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$, so the boundary $\xi = 0$, and consequently $x_1 + i y_1 = e^{i\eta}$. Thence $a_n = \cos \eta$, $y_n = \sin \eta$, and therefore $a_n^2 + y_n^2 = 1 = 0$, or the point corresponds to a point on the circumference of the circle. Also, for a point at $x = 0$, $y = 0$ in the neighbourhood of $x = 0$, $y = 0$, we have
\[
x_1 + i y_1 = e^{i n + i \eta(a + i n) + i \eta} = \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)} e^{i\eta},
\]
that is, $x = 0$, $y = 0$ corresponds to $x_1 = 0$, $y_1 = 0$ at the centre of the circle.

So far considerations as to the continuity of $\eta$ would be requisite in order to show that to the series of circles $x^2 + y^2 = e^{2\xi}$, where $\xi$ is continuously increases from $0$ to $1$, there correspond continuously enclosed curves surrounding the assumed origin and each other, continuously diminishing area, and continuously approximating to a point; that the assumed-mesh number of meshes on the boundary of $F$, and thus to complete the proof that we thus obtain an orthomorphism of $F$ into the circle, except only that to a point $x_1$, $y_1$ on the circumference of the circle there corresponds in general not a point of the contour $F$ but a length of $F$. But I abstain from a further discussion of the question.

\textbf{16.} As a simple illustration, suppose that the area $F$ is that of the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, and that the excepted point is the point $x = 0$, $y = 0$, the centre of the circle. We have here a function $\xi$ satisfying the conditions of the lemma, viz. this is $\xi = \log \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)}$; the resulting value of $\eta$ is $\eta = \tan^{-1}\frac{y}{x}$, and we have then $\xi + i\eta = \log (x + iy)$, and the resulting orthomorphosis of the two circles is the identical one
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = x + iy,
\]
viz.\ we have
\[
x_1 = x, \quad y_1 = y.
\]

% page 182
\textbf{17.} The contraction of a circle to a given eccentric point has, in fact, been exhibited in the foregoing example in No.\ 5, but I assume the question from a different point of view. Writing for shortness $a$, $a$, $a$, $a$, for a point on the conjugate function, $z = a + iy$, $\bar{z} = z - iy$, and the conjugate function $f(\bar{z})$ which give for the conjugate value of $f(\bar{z})$, where also so general formula of $(7)$ may be written in the form
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{z - a}{1 - a\bar{z}},
\]
viz.\ we have here a hyperbola, viz. its circumference $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$, $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$. In fact, repeating the verification, the change of sign of $i$ gives
\[
x_1 - iy_1 = \frac{\bar{z} - a}{1 - a z}.
\]

Here the product of the $a$-factors is
\[
\frac{(z - a)(z - a)}{1 - a(z + \bar{z}) + a^2},
\]
which, if $x^2 = 1$, reduces to $1$; the property exists, so do the $a$-factors, $\&c.$ multiplied for each. The correspondence which holds between $a$ and $\theta$ above introduced will be apparent. For these circumstances, viz.\ on putting $a = z$, we have $a = z$, and there is no real point of any importance these are no real circumferences of the circles.

\textbf{18.} The most simple case is
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{z - a}{1 - a \bar{z}}.
\]

I assume here that $a$ is real, an interior point of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ (viz. $x^2 - a^2 < 1$); hence $a = z$ gives $a$, $a$, viz. to the point $a = a$, $\nu = 0$, within the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 0$, there corresponds the centre of the circle $a_n^2 + y_n^2 = 1 = 0$, and we have in this equation the theory of the squarewise contraction of the entire circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$ to the point $x = a$, $y = 0$ being the formula become
\[
x_1 = \frac{x - a}{1 - a x}.
\]
where $a < 1$, or if in the limit $a = 1$, may be taken to be positive, then $a < 1$.

\textbf{19.} To compare with a former investigation, writing $\xi + i\eta = \log (a_n + i\eta)$, we have
\[
\xi + i\eta = \log \frac{a + i\beta a}{1 - i\beta} - \log \frac{\sqrt{((a - a) + j)}}{\sqrt{(1 - a a y + a j)}} + i \tan^{-1}\frac{y}{a - a} - i \tan^{-1}\frac{a y}{1 - a x},
\]
and consequently
\[
\xi = \log \frac{\sqrt{(a - a)^2 + g^2)}}{\sqrt{(1 - a a y + a j)}}.
\]
which is $= 0$ at the boundary $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, and has a finite and continuous throughout the area except in the neighbourhood of the point $x = a$, $y = 0$, for which it

% page 183
$= \log \sqrt{(x^2 + y^2)}$; moreover, $\xi$ satisfies the partial differential equation $\frac{d^2 \xi}{dx^2} + \frac{d^2 \xi}{dy^2} = 0$, and starting from the given value of $\xi$ we should obtain the foregoing value of $\eta$, and thence $x_1 + iy_1 = e^{\xi + i\eta}$, that is, $x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{x + iy - a}{1 - a(x + iy)}$.

\textbf{20.} As further development of the solution, writing
\[
x_1 - x_2 = \frac{x_1 - a - x(1 + a) + i y(1 - a)}{1 - a(z + iy)},
\]
that is,
\[
x_1 = \frac{x - a + iy(1 - a)}{1 - a^2(x^2 + y^2) - a^2 - a^2(x - a)}, \quad x_1 = \frac{x(1 - a^2)}{(1 - ax)^2 + a^2 y^2},
\]
and then assuming $x_1^2 + y_1^2 = \lambda^2$, we have for the contour corresponding therein
\[
x^2 - a^2 + y^2 - \lambda^2((1 - a x)^2 + a^2 y^2) = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
(x^2 + y^2) (1 - \lambda^2 a^2) - 2x(a - \lambda^2 a) + a^2 - \lambda^2 = 0,
\]
or say
\[
x^2 + y^2 - \frac{2a(1 - \lambda^2)}{1 - \lambda^2 a^2} x + \frac{a^2 - \lambda^2}{1 - \lambda^2 a^2} = 0,
\]
viz. for further development of the solution, the circle $a^2 + y^2 - 1 - 0$ is the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 - 0$ corresponding to the eccentric point $x = a$, $y = 0$.

In further development of the solution, writing
\[
\eta = \tan^{-1}\frac{y}{x - a} - \tan^{-1} \frac{ay}{1 - ax} = w,
\]
that is,
\[
\tan w = \frac{(1 - a x) y - a y (x - a)}{(1 - ax)(x - a) + a y^2},
\]
showing that the contour curves all pass through two imaginary points
\[
x = \frac{1}{a}\left(1 - \frac{1}{a}\right), \quad y = \pm\frac{i}{a}\left(\frac{1}{a} - a\right).
\]

\textbf{21.} Writing next
\[
x_1 = \frac{- x + a(1 + x^2) - a^2 x + i y - i a y - i a y - i a y}{(1 - ax)^2 + a^2 y^2},
\]
that is,
\[
x_1 = \frac{- x + a(1 + x^2) - a^2 x + i y - i a y - i a y - i a y}{(1 - ax)^2 + a^2 y^2}, \quad y_1 = \frac{y(1 - a^2)}{(1 - ax)^2 + a^2 y^2},
\]
then to the radius $x_1 - \theta y_1 = 0$ corresponds the radial
\[
- x + a + a y - \theta y (1 - a^2) = 0,
\]

% page 184
that is,
\[
(x - a)\left(a - \frac{1}{\theta}\right) + y^2 + \theta y\left(a - \frac{1}{\theta}\right) = 0,
\]
which is a circle passing through the two real points $y = 0$, $x = a$, $x = \frac{1}{a}$, being the antipodes of the before-mentioned pair of imaginary points.

\textbf{22.} Hence for the contraction of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ to the interior point $x = a$, $y = 0$, calling this point $A$, and taking $A'$ as image hereof (coordinates $x = \frac{1}{a}$, $y = 0$), the radials are circles through $A$ and $A'$, but they cut the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ at right angles; the contours are circles such that their tangents from each of the points $A$, $A'$ are equal.

In other words, the distance $AA'$ being hered (coordinates $x = \frac{1}{a}$, $y = 0$, and having thus for their common chord that line bisecting $AA'$ at right angles, the equations of the contour circles and the radial circles assume the forms
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 2hx + 1 = 0,
\]
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 2ky + 1 = 0,
\]
respectively.

\textbf{23.} It is better, interchanging $x$, $y$ and altering the constants, to write them in the form
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 2hx + 1 = 0,
\]
\[
x^2 + y^2 - 2hy - 1 = 0,
\]
viz.\ as no effect appearing in No.\ 5, these are the equations derived from $x + iy = \tan (u + iv)$ where $\tan u = a$, $\tan v = b$.

\textbf{24.} Passing now to the form
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{z - a}{1 - a z} \cdot \frac{z - \beta}{1 - \beta z},
\]
to obtain the most simple instance, I write $a = 0$, $b = \sqrt{2}$, so that the form is
\[
x_1 = \frac{i(x - \sqrt{2})}{1 - \sqrt{2}x},
\]
we then obtain
\[
x_1, \quad y_1 = \frac{x((1 - \sqrt{2}x)(x - \sqrt{2}) + y^2)}{(1 - \sqrt{2}x)^2 + 2y^2}.
\]

% page 185
that is,
\[
x_1 = \frac{x(x^2 + y^2 + 1 - 2\sqrt{2}x)}{2x^2 + y^2 - 2\sqrt{2}x + 1},
\]
and thence
\[
x_1^2 + y_1^2 = \frac{(x^2 + y^2)(x^2 + y^2 + 1 - 2\sqrt{2}x)^2 + (1 - x^2 - y^2)^2}{(x^2 + y^2)^2 - 2\sqrt{2}x(x^2 + y^2 + 1) + 2x^2 + 1},
\]
so that the circumference $x^2 + y^2 = 1$ corresponds to the two circumferences $x^2 + y^2 - 2\sqrt{2}x + 1 = 0$, $x^2 + y^2 - 2x + 1 = 0$. Whereas the values of $a$ to $a$ are below; in correspondence values for $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ should be in turn $a$
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{(\sqrt{2} - 1)i \cdot a + \sqrt{2} - 1}{x + \sqrt{2}(\sqrt{2} - 1) + i}, \quad \text{whence}, \quad x_1^2 + y_1^2 = 1.
\]
hence (the latter circle originally represented by $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$), the last separate becomes
\[
x_1^2 + y_1^2 = 1 = \frac{((\sqrt{2} + 1)y - x)^2 + y^2 - 1}{(x + 1)^2 + y^2}.
\]
$= \frac{(x + 1)^2 + y^2 - 1}{1 + (x + 1)^2 + y^2}$, $(x - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}})^2 + y^2 = \frac{1}{2}$,
(this rectangle differs only in position from the first-mentioned rectangle $ABCD$); we have an orthomorphism of a circle (the radius of which may be taken to be $-1$) and the infinite half-plane $a$, $-0$, into a circle of, $1 - 1$, $1$ this circle being $a$ symmetrical to the both planes. In figure 3, in the first place, the quasi-inversion is a symmetrical one will be subjected to the two-bend system as a symmetrical to the rectangle, but in this case which the two transformations\footnote{I made a transformation in figure 2, where the quasi-inversion is consequence of which after which a constant follows in $y$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$ to $A$, $B$, $D$, $\&c$.}

After a time losing the indentation, and approximating more and more to the form of a circle having for its centre the point $x = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, $y = 0$, which is the centre of the circle, and ultimately reducing itself to this point.

It will be noticed that the consecutive contour $S$ as shown in the figure is not an infinitesimal distance from every part of the circle which is the contour $S$; for a part of the circle, the transverse contour or contour $A$ at an infinitesimal distance is by part of the symmetrically situated oval, lying outside of the circle. And thus in the present case we do obtain a contraction of the circle into the contour $S$; that is, in fact, the oval rules, the consecutive contour around the next consecutive circles which are part of the conditions of the lemma.

\textbf{27.} For the radials, writing as before $x + i \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ in place of $x$, that is, $x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ in place of $x$, we have
\[
x_1 = z + i \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, \quad y_1 = \frac{a((x + a)^2 + y^2)}{a^2 + y^2},
\]
Hence
\[
x_1 = z + i \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, \quad y_1 = \frac{a((x + a)^2 + y^2)}{a^2 + y^2},
\]
\[
y_1 = z + i \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, \quad y_1 = \frac{a((x + a)^2 + y^2)}{a^2 + y^2},
\]
and thus to the radius $x_1 - \theta y_1 = 0$ there corresponds the curve
\[
x(x^2 + y^2 - a^2) - \theta a(x^2 + y^2 + a^2) = 0,
\]
or say
\[
(x - \theta a)(x^2 + y^2) = (x + \theta a) a^2,
\]
a circular cubic. In particular, for the line $x_1 = 0$, we have $\theta = 0$, and the curve is
\[
x(x^2 + y^2 - a^2) = 0,
\]
viz.\ this consists of the line $x = 0$, and of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - a^2 = 0$. We thus have for one of the radials, the line $x = 0$, that is, the chord which is real, the radials of figure 3.

The curve passes through the origin $x = 0$, $y = 0$, and the equation of the

% page 187
tangent there is $x + \theta y = 0$. Moreover it passes through the centre of the circle, $x = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, $y = 0$, and the equation of the tangent there is easily found to be
\[
x - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} - \theta y = 0,
\]
the equation of the line being
\[
\left(x - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) - \theta y = 0,
\]
We may find where the curve cuts the circle $\left(x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^2 + y^2 - \frac{1}{2} = 0$, viz.\ this is $x^2 + y^2 - \sqrt{2}x = 0$, and substituting in the equation of the curve, we have
\[
x(\sqrt{2}x - a) - \theta a(\sqrt{2}x + a)
\]
or say
\[
\left(x - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)(x^2 + y^2) - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta y(x^2 + y^2 - 2 \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x) = 0,
\]
a hyperbola which for its intersections with the circle determines the points in question. There are two real intersections, but if it is obvious that we are bounding the unshaded portion of the value of curves of which only a part is shown by quadrature of the intersection of the circle with the other branch of the cubic determines the contour cycle. There must thus be a circle (one of the contour cycles being a square) and shown in Plate XIII.

\textbf{28.} The formula
\[
x_1 = \frac{x + a}{1 - ax}
\]
has been considered for the case of an interior point $a$, the case of an exterior point $a$ might be considered in like manner, but it is obvious that we pass from one to the other, by a transformation $\frac{1}{a}$ for $a$; and it thus easily appears that, if $a$ be an exterior point, viz. when $a^2 > 1$ that is, the exterior point, since this can be by the transformation $\frac{1}{a}$ to $a$, reduced to that of two interior points, but it is sufficient to consider this last case.

\textbf{29.} Considering then the form
\[

\]
\[
= \frac{(x^2 + y^2 - a^2)^2 + 4a^2 y^2}{1 - a^2(x^2 + y^2)^2 - 4a^2 y^2},
\]
which has for the circumference $x^2 + y^2 = 1$ the value $= 1$, the imaginary curve $x^2 + y^2 = 0$ corresponds to that of the unshaded curve. Writing now $a_n^2 + y_n^2 = w^2$ we have
\[
(x^2 + y^2 - a^2)^2 + 4a^2 y^2 = w^2 \{(1 - a^2(x^2 + y^2)^2) + 4a^2 y^2\},

a bicircular quartic which is of the figure, dividing, dissolving with $a$, and after some changes reducing itself to the form of a figure of eight in squarewise consider to the circle $a_n^2 + y_n^2 - a^2 = 0$. For $a = 1$, the curve breaks up into the two circles $a_n^2 + y_n^2 - 1 = 0$ and into the infinite curve $a^2 + y^2 = 1$, doubling the two circumferences

\textbf{24.} The Schwarzian orthomorphosis of the square into a circle is given (Memoir, p. 113) by the formula $X_1 + iY_1 = m (X + iY)$, where the modulus is $k = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, which corresponds to the angle $45^\circ$.

% page 189
and for the circle, they are two sets of bicircular quartics, such that the portions within the circle have a configuration resembling that of the confocal ellipses and hyperbolas within the ellipse. To investigate the formula, it is convenient to introduce the function
\[
X + iY, = \sin^{-1}(s + ig),
\]
we then have
\[
x + iy = \sin (X + iY),
\]
or say
\[
x = \sin X \cosh Y, \quad y = \cos X \sinh Y,
\]
so that to any given value of $Y$ there corresponds the curve
\[
\frac{x^2}{\cosh^2 Y} + \frac{y^2}{\sinh^2 Y} = 1,
\]
and to
\[
x = \sin X, \quad y = -\cos X, \quad \text{or}, \quad x_n = \sin X, \quad y_n = \cos X,
\]
\[
x = \sin X, \quad y = \cos X, \quad x_n = \cos X, \quad y_n = \sin X,
\]
\[
\theta = \sin X, \quad y = -\sin X (1 + iY),
\]
then
\[
x_1 = \sin X \cos iY, \quad y_1 = \cos X \sin iY,
\]
giving
\[
\frac{x^2}{\cos^2 X} - \frac{y^2}{\sinh^2 Y} = 1, \text{ if } 1 - x_n^2 = 0,, \text{ that is, } X = (k + \frac{1}{2})\pi, \text{ or since}
\]
\[
\cos X = \sqrt{2} \cos X = \cos \cos X = \sqrt{2} \cos X
\]
\[
\sin Y = \sqrt{1 - \cos^2 X}, \quad \frac{dY}{dX} = \frac{c X}{\sqrt{1 - c^2 X^2}}, \text{ or } K = \frac{x^2 K}{a^2},
\]
hence defining $a$ as above, it appears that the elliptic polygons $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ corresponds to the circumference $a_n^2 + y_n^2 = 1$. The aside Quartic and the graphical representation of the Elliptic Functions,'' Comb. Phil. Trans., t.\ \textsc{xiv}.\ (1889), pp.\ 105---120 (see p. 113) for the orthomorphosis of others into a circle.

\textbf{25.} The whole theory, and in particular Riemann's theorem before referred to,

% page 190
are intimately connected with Cauchy's theorem, ``If a function $f(z)$ is holomorphic over a simply connected plane area, and if $z$ denote any point within the area, then
\[
f(z) = \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int \frac{f(z) dz}{z - z'},
\]
where $z$ denotes $z = i\eta$, and the integral is taken in the positive sense along the boundary of the area.'' See Briot and Bouquet, \textit{Th\'{e}orie des fonctions elliptiques} (Paris, 1875), p. 136.

Here in order to obtain by means of the theorem the value of the function $f(z)$ for a given point of the boundary; viz. if $z$ refers to a point $P$ on the boundary, and $z$ to a second point $P_n$ in the boundary, but also the several points of $f(z) dz$, the form of this boundary, but also the several points $P$, throughout which correspond to the several points $P$ of the first-mentioned boundary; or say we require to know the correspondence of the two boundaries: this being known, we have by the theorem the value of $f(z)$, that is, the point $a_n + i$, within the first area, which corresponds to the point $z$ on the boundary, as a determinate value, determined by means of the given values of $f(z)$ along the boundary.

% ====================================================================
% PAPER 921: ON SOME PROBLEMS OF ORTHOMORPHOSIS
% ====================================================================
\newpage
\paper{921}{ON SOME PROBLEMS OF ORTHOMORPHOSIS}{From Crelle's Journal der Mathem., t.~cviii. (1891), pp.~263---277.}{}

In the interesting Memoir, Schwarz, ``Ueber einige Abbildungsaufgaben,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.\ \textsc{lxx}.\ (1869), pp.\ 105---120, the author considers the orthomorphic transformation (or, as I call it, the orthomorphosis) of a square into the infinite half-plane, or into a circle, and of a rectangle into the same. It is of course easy to deduce the orthomorphosis of the rectangle into a circle; and thus, by giving a proper value to the modulus of the elliptic function involved in the formula, we obtain the orthomorphosis of the square into a circle, this solution (although equivalent thereto) being under a different form, and accompanied with a rectangle, and (although not necessary) considering for a moment the rectangle as a square, but to the circumference together with two two-cusped portions of a diameter. I propose to consider in I, these several transformations: II. relates to the orthomorphosis of a circle into a circle.

\textbf{1.} I.\ We are concerned with the elliptic functions for which $K' = 2K$, and also with the elliptic function $sn$ of the lemniscatic form. The modulus in the former case is
\[
k = \frac{\sqrt{2} - 1}{\sqrt{2} + 1} = (\sqrt{2} - 1)^2; \quad k^2 = \sqrt{2}; \quad k^2 = -1 + 2\sqrt{2}; \quad \text{or say } k\sqrt{2} = \sqrt{2} + 1.
\]

For the lemniscatic form, the modulus is $= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, and (with Gauss) we write $\frac{1}{2}\omega$ for the value of the complete function $K$ or $F_n$; thus we have $\frac{1}{2}\omega = K(\sqrt{2} - 1)$.

% page 192
$= K' \sqrt{2}$, where $k$ has the foregoing special value: I notice the numerical values $\frac{1}{2}\omega = 1.311029$, $K = 3 + 2\sqrt{2} \cdot \frac{1}{2}\omega \cdot p = 1.682245$. The relation between the lemniscate function, and the $sn$ with the foregoing value of $k$, is easily shown to be
\[
\sqrt{2} sn U = \frac{(c + 1) sin l \cdot a \sqrt{2}}{(c - 1) sin l \cdot a + \sqrt{2}} = 1.582245,
\]
and it may be added that we have
\[
sn U - \frac{\sqrt{2}}{(c - 1) sin l \cdot a + \sqrt{2}} = \frac{1}{2} sin l \cdot a (1 - cml \cdot a)),
\]
\[
dn U = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{(c - 1) sin l \cdot a + \sqrt{2}} \cdot \sqrt{1 + a \cdot (- cml \cdot a)} \cdot (1 + cml \cdot a),
\]

\textbf{2.} The Schwarzian orthomorphosis of the rectangle into the infinite half-plane is given (Memoir, p. 113) by the formula $X_1 + iY_1 = \sin (X + iY)$, where the modulus is such that $K = 2K'$; viz. here---see figure 1 (.X, Y) and figure 2 (.x, y)---the rectangle $ABCD$, the half-diagonals whereof are each $= \frac{1}{2}\pi$, corresponds to the upper half-plane $a_n = 0$; in fact, $X_n = a$, $Y_n = 0$, where $\sin(X) = 0$, gives $x_n = \sin X$, $y_n = 0$. We have so that the entire side $4$ of the rectangle $ABCD$ corresponds to the entire side $D, C$ of the value of $a$, between the limits $a^2 = - 1$, $a^2 = + 1$, where (for the side $A, B$ at this half-plane $y_n = 0$).

% page 193
$X_n$ has $a$ and $b$ symmetrically, in regard to the axis $OY_n$ and in the case of the before-mentioned modulus $k = \sqrt{2} - 2 \cdot \sqrt{2}$ for which the rectangle $ABCD$ becomes a square. \textit{Fig.}\ 1$^a$ $(X, Y_n)$.

square, the quasi-inversion may be so determined that the points $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ shall lie situate ortho (that is, in inclinations $\pm 45^\circ$, $\pm 135^\circ$) to the axis $OY_n$ in the half-mentioned manner of figure 1, the four points $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$, $A$, $A B$ of the centre of these circles, then forming $A$, $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$, as in the figure (it is for the figure of the lemniscate), it is at once obvious that the boundary of the rectangle is a symmetrical one, will be situated, $A$ and $B$ symmetrically, and similarly for $p'$ and $b'$. But we have
\[
b = c \cdot \sqrt{2}\left(\frac{1}{a^2} - 1\right) = \sqrt{2}(c - a)\sqrt{2}, \quad c = \sqrt{2}c + 1,
\]

% page 194
\textbf{6.} The general formula of the inversion whereby the rectangle $ABCD$-axis is changed into a circumference is changed
\[
X_1 + iY_1 = \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)} \cdot \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)} \cdot \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)} \cdot \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)},
\]
where $M$ is real. In fact, here this equation in the form
\[
X_1 + iY_1 = \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)},
\]
where $M$ is real. In fact, here this equation in the form
\[
X_1 + iY_1 = \frac{1 + i\sqrt{X(X_1 + iY_1)}}{M(X_1 + iY_1)},
\]
we have
\[
X_1 = iY_1 = \frac{1 - i\sqrt{X(X_1 - iY_1)}}{M(X_1 - iY_1)},
\]
and thence $X_1^2 + Y_1^2 = 1 = 0$, that is, the infinite $X_1$-axis is transformed into the circumference $X_1^2 + Y_1^2 = 0$.

Writing $Y_n = 0$, we have
\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + ia(X_n + iY_n)}{M(X_n - iY_n)},
\]
so that to a pair of points $(X_n, 0)$, there corresponds a pair of points $(X_n, Y_n)$ symmetrically in regard to the axis $OY_n$.

The equation gives
\[
M(X_n + iY_n) = (X_n + iY_n)(X_n - iY_n) - i(X_n + iY_n)
\]
hence, writing this in the form
\[
M(X_n + iY_n) = \frac{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)}{1 + i(X_n - iY_n)} \cdot \frac{(X_n + iY_n)}{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)},
\]

% page 195
we have
\[
M(X_n - iY_n) = \frac{1 + i(X_n - iY_n)}{1 - i(X_n - iY_n)} \cdot \frac{(X_n - iY_n)}{1 - i(X_n - iY_n)},
\]
and consequently $M^2(X_n^2 + Y_n^2) = 1$, if only $Y_n = 0$; that is, the circumference $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = 0$ is transformed into the infinite $X_n$-axis.

Although for the purposes of the present memoir we require the coefficient $M$, yet there is no real loss of generality in assuming $M = 1$, and the transformation is then
\[
X_1 + iY_1 = \frac{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)}{1 - i(X_n + iY_n)};
\]
not post No.\ 12.

\textbf{7.} As already mentioned, the modulus $(X_n, Y_n)$ and $(X_n, Y_n)$ are connected by an equation of the foregoing form, and in the case of the square $(k = \sqrt{2}/2)$ as before, the corresponding values for the points $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ may be tabulated as follows:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
& $X_n + iY_n$ & $X_n + iY_n$ \\
\hline
$A$, & $X_n + iY_n = -$, & $X_n + iY_n = \frac{- 1 + i}{\sqrt{2}}$, \\
$B$, & $X_n + iY_n = -1$, & $X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 - i}{\sqrt{2}}$, \\
$C$, & $X_n + iY_n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, & $X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + i}{\sqrt{2}}$, \\
$D$, & $X_n + iY_n = -1$, & $X_n + iY_n = \frac{- 1 - i}{\sqrt{2}}$. \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The proper value of $M$ is $M = \sqrt{2} - 1$, viz. the required formula is
\[
\sqrt{2} \cdot (X_1 + iY_1) = \frac{1 - i(X_n + iY_n)}{X_n + iY_n - 1}.
\]
Thus for the point $B$, we should have $\sqrt{2}$, that is, $\sqrt{2} \cdot \frac{1 - i}{\sqrt{2} + 1} \cdot \sqrt{2 - 1} = \frac{(1 - i)\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{2} - 1)}{1 - i\sqrt{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{2} - i}{1 - i\sqrt{2}}$
which is right. And similarly for the point $C$, we should have
\[
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \cdot \frac{1 - i(1 + i)\sqrt{2}}{(1 + i)\sqrt{2} - 1} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \cdot \frac{1 - i + 1}{1 - 1 + 1 + i\sqrt{2}} = \frac{2 - i}{1 + i\sqrt{2}}, \quad \frac{1 + i}{\sqrt{2}},
\]
which is right. And similarly for the points $A$ and $D$.

\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)}{X_n + iY_n - 1};
\]

in fact, recollecting that $i (X_n + i Y_n) = K_n$, $x_n = sn (a + iv)$, we have for the four points respectively
\[
A, \quad X_n + iY_n = K_n, \quad a + iv = K + iK_n,
\]
\[
B, \quad X_n + iY_n = - K, \quad a + iv = - K + iK_n,
\]
\[
C, \quad X_n + iY_n = K + iK_n, \quad a + iv = K,
\]
\[
D, \quad X_n + iY_n = - K + iK_n, \quad a + iv = - K,
\]
values which satisfy the relation in question.

\textbf{9.} Hence writing
\[
X + iY = U, \quad x + iy = u,
\]
we have
\[
U = \frac{1 + iu}{1 - iu},
\]
which is the relation in No.\ 1 between the arguments $U$, $u$ of the elliptic functions $sn$, $cl$. We have $X_n + iY_n = cl (u + iv)$, and consequently
\[
\sqrt{2}(X_n + iY_n) = \frac{1 - i \cdot cl (u + iv)}{cl (u + iv) - 1},
\]

Hence, substituting the value of $X_n + iY_n$, we find that, $X_n + iY_n$,

an equation which (multiplying on the left-hand side the numerator and the denominator each by $\frac{- 1}{\sqrt{2}}$) may be written
\[
\frac{1 - i \cdot cl (u + iv) + i \frac{- i}{\sqrt{2}}}{1 + i \cdot \frac{- i}{\sqrt{2}}(u + iv) - 1} = \frac{1 - i(X_n + iY_n)}{X_n + iY_n - 1};
\]
that is, we have
\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + i \cdot cl (u + iv)}{cl (u + iv) - 1},
\]
an equation which shows that the figure $A$ is in fact the figure 4 turned through an angle of $45^\circ$. We have thus proved the conclusion stated in No.\ 5 as to the connexion between the lemniscatic relation by the spaces equal in the relation, viz.\ from the rectangle.

\textbf{10.} It is convenient to collect here the several equations relating to the orthomorphosis of the square. We have
\[
X_n + iY_n = sn (X + iY), \quad k = \sqrt{2} - 1;
\]
\[
a_n + (a_n = sn(a + iv);
\]
\[
\sqrt{2}(X_n + iY_n) = \frac{1 - i(X_n + iY_n)}{X_n + iY_n - 1};
\]
\[
\sqrt{2} \cdot a_n - \frac{- 1 - i a_n}{X_n + iY_n - 1};
\]
\[
X_n + iY_n - \frac{1 + ia_n}{1 - ia_n};
\]
\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)}{1 - i(X_n + iY_n)},
\]
which are the equations connecting together the coordinates of the four figures.

\textbf{11.} I examine more in detail the above-mentioned transformation
\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{1 + i(X_n + iY_n)}{X_n + iY_n - 1},
\]
or, the foregoing figures 1 (X) and 2 (X, Y, n), in which we may regard the two circles as having each of them the same radius unity, changing the sign of $i$, the equation gives
\[
X_n - iY_n = \frac{1 - i(X_n - iY_n)}{X_n - iY_n - 1},
\]
and we hence find
\[
X + Y = \frac{X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n - 1}{X_n^2 + Y_n^2 - 2X_n + 1},
\]
consequently if $X^2 + Y^2 = 1$, then also $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = 0$; or the transformation changes the form of these imaginary circles into the second of them: that is, the orthomorphosis of the circle $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = 0$ into the concentric orthomorphosis of the circle $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = 1$. Or we have
\[
X_n^2 + Y_n^2 - 2X_n + 1 - 0, \quad X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1 - 0,
\]
values which give the foregoing expression for $X^2 + Y^2$. We can no further obtain
\[
X^2 + Y^2 - 1 = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1}, \quad Y_n = \frac{X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1}{X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1} = 0;
\]
and it then appears that the circumferences $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = 0$ and $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 - 1 = 0$ correspond to each other.

% page 198
correspond to each other. These are circles passing through the pairs of points $(Y_n = 0, X_n = \pm 1)$, $(Y_n = 0, X_n = \pm 1)$ respectively; or imagining them in the same figure, say they are circles which belong each to the centre of the figure, with the line $X_n = 0$, and which moreover cut at right angles the circles $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1 = 0$, $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 + 2X_n + 1 = 0$. But attending more carefully to the nature of the correspondence, it is to be observed that taking $X$ positive, and not at the value of $a_n = \pm 1$, then the corresponding more on the upper half-circle from $a_n = \pm 1$ to $a_n + 1$ on the upper semicircle $LFM$; and that, corresponding hereto in figure 2, as are $LJM$ lying wholly within the lower semicircle $LDM$; and, that, corresponding herein in figure 2, as are $LFM$ on $LM$ to the diameter $LM$ of the first circles $LMF$; that is, the upper half-plane bounded below by the upper semicircle $LFM$ corresponds to the lower semicircle $LDM$ in the first figure; that is, the upper half-plane bounded below by the lower semicircle $LDM$. Thus the upper semicircle $LFM$ of figure 2 corresponds to the lower semicircle $LDM$ of figure 1; and as for the lower semicircle $LDM$ of figure 3, the upper half-plane bounded below by the lower semicircle $LDM$ in figure 2, and below by the lower semicircle $LDM$ in figure 2, are belonging wholly inside the upper semicircle $LJM$; that is, in figure 2, are by figure 3, the upper semicircle $LFM$ of figure 2; corresponding to the semicircle $LJM$ of figure 3 corresponding to the semicircumference $LDM$ of figure 1, viz. the lower semicircle $LDM$ of figure 1 corresponds to the upper semicircle $LDM$ of figure 3; the upper half-plane bounded by the lower semicircle $LDM$ in figure 2 corresponds to the lower part of the upper half-plane bounded above by the lower semicircle $LDM$ in figure 3; the upper half-plane $a_n = 0$ here corresponds to the upper half-plane of the upper half-plane bounded by the upper semicircle $LJM$ in figure 3.

\textbf{12.} In explanation of No.\ 4, observe that, starting from the equation
\[
X_n + iY_n = m(X + iY), \quad k = \sqrt{2} + 1,
\]
and writing $m X = P$, $m i Y = i Q$ (the analytical formula obtains for any real value of $k$), and assuming a system of values $X^2 = 1$ and $X^2 = 1$ each passing through the points $X = 0, m = 1$, and these of the other set through the antipodes $X_n = \pm 1, Y_n = 0$.

This being so, the equation of the two circles is
\[
a^2 + y^2 - 2p, \cos \mu, \quad p_1 = 1,
\]
\[
X_n + iY_n = \frac{P \sqrt{1 + Q^2 \cdot 1 + k^2 Q^2} + iQ \sqrt{1 + P^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 P^2}}{1 + k^2 P^2 Q^2},
\]
that is,
\[
X_n = \frac{P \sqrt{1 + Q^2 \cdot 1 + k^2 Q^2}}{1 + k^2 P^2 Q^2}, \quad Y_n = \frac{Q \sqrt{1 + P^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 P^2}}{1 + k^2 P^2 Q^2}.
\]

% page 199
and thence
\[
X_n + Y_n = \frac{P + iQ}{1 + k^2 P^2 Q^2} \cdot (X_n + Y_n \text{ be put } = mP);
\]
hence
\[
P^2 (1 - k^2 P^2) m^2 = -Q^2 \cdot Q^2 (1 - k^2 P^2) m^2 = - P.
\]

Now considering in figure 1 say line in the rectangle parallel to the axis of $X$, that is, taking $Y$ constant and therefore also $Q$ constant, and proceeding to eliminate $P$, we have
\[
1 - P^2 = \frac{1 + k^2 Q^2 (1 - m^2)}{1 - k^2 m^2 Q^2}, \quad 1 - k^2 P^2 = \frac{1 + k^2 Q^2 (1 - m^2)}{1 - k^2 m^2 Q^2}, \quad 1 + k^2 P^2 Q^2 = \frac{1 + k^2 Q^2}{1 - k^2 m^2 Q^2},
\]
and consequently
\[
X_n = \sqrt{1 + Q^2 \cdot 1 + k^2 Q^2} \cdot \frac{1 + Q^2 (1 - k^2 m^2 Q^2)}{1 + k^2 Q^2},
\]
\[
Y_n = \frac{Q \sqrt{1 + Q^2(1 - k^2 m^2)} \sqrt{1 + Q^2 - 2 k Y (1 + Q^2)}}{1 + k^2 Q^2},
\]
giving $Y_n^2 + 1 = \frac{1}{m^2}$ of these in terms of $Q$ and $m$, $X_n^2 + Y_n^2$. The former of these equations, replacing thereby $\eta^2$ by its value, gives
\[
(X_n^2 + Y_n^2 - 2k X_n - 2 k Y_n) + \frac{Q^2}{m^2} = 0,
\]
viz.\ we have thus the equation of the curve, a bicircular quartic which in figure 2 corresponds to the line parallel to the axis of $X$ in figure 1.

In particular, for the line $LM$ of figure 1, we have $Y = \frac{1}{k}$ and thence $Q = \frac{1}{km}$; that is, $Q = \frac{1}{k}$, and hence we have $\Delta = \frac{1}{k}$: the equation of the bicircular quartic is
\[
(X_n^2 + Y_n^2)^2 = \frac{2}{k} (X_n + Y_n) + \frac{1}{m^2} = 0;
\]
viz.\ this is the circle $X_n^2 + Y_n^2 = \frac{1}{k}$ twice repeated, and we have then this circle, or rather the half-circumference $LFM$ of figure 1, corresponding to the line $LM$ of figure 1. More precisely, to the line $CD$ of figure 4, there corresponds the quarter-circumference $CD$

% page 200
and thence
\[
X_n + Y_n = \frac{(1 + i P)\sqrt{P^2 - 1} - (1 + i P)\sqrt{1 + k^2 P^2}}{4(1 + k^2 P^2)(1 + i Q)},
\]
that is, $X_n + Y_n = 0$ as before. It is easy to see that the points $A$, $O$, $B$ of figure 1 correspond to the points $A$, $O$, $B$ of figure 2, and hence that the area of the rectangle $AOBFEA$ of figure 1 corresponds to that of the semicircle $AOBFEA$ of figure 2.

Returning to the question $X_n + iY_n = m (X + iY)$, if we write therein successively $m = \alpha$ and $m = \beta$, we obtain two values of the rectangle $AOBFEA$ in figure 2, parallel to $AB$, equal in their successive positions ($\alpha$, $\beta$), which we have in their successive horizontal positions inside the rectangle. We have moreover, in like manner, similarly for the parallel sides $AD$ and $BC$ as cause of which, viz., similar curves are the parallel sides of the rectangle. \textit{The points $A$, $O$, $B$ correspond to the cusps $A$, $O$, $B$ of the same curve}, viz. of figure 2, that is, the cusps in the inner branches of the curve corresponding to $AOBFEA$ in figure 1, and so on.

\textbf{13.} Similarly in explanation of No.\ 5, observe that, starting from the equation
\[
x_1 + i y_1 = ml (x + iy) \text{ and writing } onl = x, \text{ } ml = y \text{ and } y \text{ } = m y, \text{ we have}
\]
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{P\sqrt{1 - Q^2}\sqrt{1 + Q^2} + iQ\sqrt{1 + P^2}\sqrt{1 - P^2}}{1 + P^2 Q^2},
\]
that is,
\[
x_1 + iy_1 = \frac{P\sqrt{1 - Q^2}\sqrt{1 + Q^2}}{1 + P^2 Q^2}, \quad y_1 = \frac{Q\sqrt{1 + P^2}\sqrt{1 - P^2}}{1 + P^2 Q^2},
\]
and thence
\[
x_1^2 + y_1^2 = \frac{P^2 + Q^2}{1 + P^2 Q^2}, \quad x_1^2 - y_1^2 = \frac{P^2 - Q^2 - P^2 Q^4 + Q^2 P^4}{(1 + P^2 Q^2)^2},
\]
writing $x + iy = \frac{1}{2}\omega$, we have
\[
sn \frac{1}{2}\omega = \frac{P\sqrt{1 - Q^2}}{\sqrt{1 + P^2 Q^2}}, \quad cn \frac{1}{2}\omega = \frac{\sqrt{1 - P^2}}{\sqrt{1 - Q^2}}, \quad dn \frac{1}{2}\omega = \frac{\sqrt{1 + P^2 - 1}}{\sqrt{1 + Q^2}}, \quad dn \frac{1}{2}\omega = \sqrt{1 + Q^2},
\]
the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ is transformed into the circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 = 1$. More precisely, to the line $CD$ of figure 4, there corresponds the quarter-circumference $CD$

\section*{921. On some Problems of Orthomorphosis.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}921. On some Problems of Orthomorphosis.}

[From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions}, vol.~CLXXXIII (1892), pp.~653--669.]

\bigskip

\textbf{14.} To any line in figure 4, parallel to the axis $Ox$ or to the axis $Oy$, there
corresponds in figure 5 a bicircular quartic of the form $x^2 + y^2 - 2Ax^2 - 2By^2 - 1 = 0$.
The investigation is substantially the same as that contained in No.~12, and need
not be here given. But it is remarkable that also, to any line of figure 4 parallel
to a side of the square (that is, to any line $x \pm y = c$), there corresponds in figure 5
a bicircular quartic of the like form (for the sides of the square, or lines $x \pm y = \pm \sqrt{2}$
of figure 4, this bicircular quartic becomes the twice repeated circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$
of figure 5, which is the result just obtained in No.~13). I investigate this result
as follows. Writing, as in No.~13,
\[
\operatorname{sn} x = p, \quad \operatorname{sn} iy = i\, \operatorname{sn} y = iq,
\]
we have, as above,
\[
x = \frac{p\sqrt{1-q^4} + q\sqrt{1-p^4}}{1-p^2q^2},
\quad
y = \frac{p\sqrt{1-q^4} - q\sqrt{1-p^4}}{1-p^2q^2},
\]
and thence
\[
x_1 = \frac{p\sqrt{1-q^4} + q\sqrt{1-p^4}}{1-p^2q^2},
\]
where $x_1 = x + y$ and $y_1 = x - y$.

Now assuming between $x$ and $y$ the relation $x + y = C$, and writing $\operatorname{sn} C = c$, this gives
\[
x_1 = \frac{p\sqrt{1-q^4} + q\sqrt{1-p^4}}{1-p^2q^2}.
\]
To obtain the required curve, we must between these equations (three independent
equations) eliminate $p$ and $q$. We have
\[
x_1 + y_1 = \frac{2p\sqrt{1-q^4}}{1-p^2q^2},
\quad
x_1 - y_1 = \frac{2q\sqrt{1-p^4}}{1-p^2q^2},
\]
and consequently, writing for convenience $n = 1 - p^2q^2$,
\[
nx_1 = p\sqrt{1-q^4}, \quad ny_1 = q\sqrt{1-p^4};
\]
these equations may be written
\[
p^2 = \frac{x_1^2}{n^2 + x_1^2}, \quad q^2 = \frac{y_1^2}{n^2 + y_1^2},
\]
and from these equations obtaining the expressions for $p^2$ and $q^2$, and thence the
expression for $p^2q^2 = 1 - n$, we find, after some easy reductions,
\[
n^2 - n(x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1) - x_1^2y_1^2 = 0.
\]
But we have
\[
x_1^2 + y_1^2 = \frac{2(p^2 + q^2 - 2p^2q^2)}{(1-p^2q^2)^2},
\]
or substituting this value, the equation becomes
\[
\bigl[(x_1^2 + y_1^2)^2 - 2c^2(x_1^2 + y_1^2) + c^4\bigr] + (1-c^2)\bigl[(x_1^2 + y_1^2)^2 - c^2(x_1^2 + y_1^2)\bigr] - 4c^2x_1^2y_1^2 = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
(x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1)^2 + (1-c^2)(x_1^2 + y_1^2 - c^2)(x_1^2 - y_1^2) = 0;
\]
the required equation. Transforming through an angle of $45^\circ$ by writing
\[
x_2 = \frac{x_1 + y_1}{\sqrt{2}}, \quad y_2 = \frac{x_1 - y_1}{\sqrt{2}}
\]
(where observe that the axis of $x_2$ is the line $FH$ of figure 5), the equation becomes
\[
(x_2^2 + y_2^2 - 1)^2 + (1-c^2)(2x_2^2 - 2y_2^2) = 0,
\]
or writing $c = \cos\gamma$ and therefore $1 - c^2 = \sin^2\gamma$, this equation becomes
\[
(x_2^2 + y_2^2)^2 - 2(x_2^2\cos^2\gamma + y_2^2\sin^2\gamma) + 1 = 0,
\]
a curve consisting of two indented ovals situate symmetrically in regard to the axis
$Fy_2$ of figure 5. In fact, writing in the equation $y_2 = 0$, we have for $x_2^2$ two real
positive values; but, writing $x_2 = 0$, we have for $y_2^2$ two imaginary values. For $\gamma=0$,
the equation becomes
\[
(x_2^2 + y_2^2)^2 - 2(x_2^2 + y_2^2) + 1 = 0,
\]
that is, we have the circle $x_2^2 + y_2^2 - 1 = 0$ twice repeated. One of the ovals is
shown in figure 5; the portion of it lying within the circle agrees with Schwarz's
figure, p.~113, turning this round through an angle of $45^\circ$.

For the lines $x - y = C$ of figure 4, we have in figure 5 the same system of
bicircular quartics turned round through an angle of $90^\circ$.

\bigskip
\textbf{II.}

\textbf{15.} I consider the general problem of the orthomorphosis of a circle into a
circle: we can, for the transformation of the circumference of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$
into that of the circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$, find a formula involving an arbitrary function
or (what is the same thing) an indefinite number of arbitrary constants. In fact,
writing for shortness $z = x + iy$, $z_1 = x_1 + iy_1$, and $\bar{z}$, $\bar{z}_1$ for the conjugate functions
$x - iy$, $x_1 - iy_1$; also $\phi(z)$ for a function of $z$ involving in general imaginary coefficients
$a + ib$, \&c., and $\bar{\phi}(\bar{z})$ for the like function with the conjugate coefficients
$a - ib$, \&c.; then if we assume
\[
z_1 = \frac{\phi(z)}{\bar{\phi}(\bar{z})} \cdot \frac{1}{z^m},
\]
where $m$ is any positive or negative integer, this implies
\[
\bar{z}_1 = \frac{\bar{\phi}(\bar{z})}{\phi(z)} \cdot \frac{1}{\bar{z}^m};
\]
consequently, if $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$, that is, $z\bar{z} = 1$, or $\bar{z} = \frac{1}{z}$, we have
\[
z_1\bar{z}_1 = \frac{1}{z^m\bar{z}^m} = 1,
\]
or $z_1\bar{z}_1 = 1$, that is, $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$. \hfill [(1)]

In a slightly different form, taking
\[
\phi(z) = (z - \alpha)(z - \beta)\ldots,
\]
$\alpha$, $\beta$, \&c., to denote any imaginary quantities,
and $\bar{\alpha}$, $\bar{\beta}$, \ldots\ the conjugate quantities; assuming
\[
\phi(z) = (z - \alpha)(z - \beta) \ldots,
\]
and taking $m$ for the number of factors, we have
\[
z_1 = \frac{(z - \alpha)(z - \beta)\ldots}{(\bar{z} - \bar{\alpha})(\bar{z} - \bar{\beta})\ldots} \cdot \frac{1}{z^m},
\]
and then (repeating the demonstration) we have
\[
z_1 = \frac{(z - \alpha)(z - \beta)\ldots}{(\frac{1}{z} - \bar{\alpha})(\frac{1}{z} - \bar{\beta})\ldots} \cdot \frac{1}{z^m},
\]
which, writing therein $\bar{z} = \frac{1}{z}$, becomes
\[
z_1 = \frac{(z - \alpha)(z - \beta)\ldots}{(1 - \bar{\alpha}z)(1 - \bar{\beta}z)\ldots},
\]
and consequently, if $z\bar{z} = 1$, then also $z_1\bar{z}_1 = 1$ as before.

We may in the expression for $z_1$ introduce a factor $\frac{A}{\bar{A}}$, or, what is the same
thing, a factor $A$ which is such that $A\bar{A} = 1$. In particular, we thus have the solution
\[
z_1 = A\frac{z - \alpha}{1 - \bar{\alpha}z}.
\]

\newpage
\textbf{16.} This is a solution with three arbitrary constants, viz.~$A$ (which may be
put $= \cos\lambda + i\sin\lambda$) is a single arbitrary constant, and $\alpha = a + ib$, is two arbitrary
constants; and these constants may be so determined that a given point in the
interior of the one circle, and a given point on the circumference thereof, shall
correspond respectively to a given point in the interior of the other circle and to a
given point on the circumference thereof. According to a well-known theorem of
Riemann's, any two simply connected areas included within given closed curves
respectively may be made to correspond to each other, and that in one way only,
under the foregoing conditions as to a pair of interior points and a pair of boundary
points: and we have, in what just precedes, the solution of the problem in the case
of two equal circles.

\textbf{17.} In the case of any other solution, we thus know that the correspondence
between the two circumferences cannot and does not imply a $(1, 1)$ correspondence
between the areas of the two circles: but it is interesting to enquire what happens.
I take a very particular case
\[
z_1 = \frac{z(z-2)}{1-2z},
\]
and therefore
\[
\bar{z}_1 = \frac{\bar{z}(\bar{z}-2)}{1-2\bar{z}},
\]
and consequently
\[
z_1\bar{z}_1 = \frac{z\bar{z}(z-2)(\bar{z}-2)}{(1-2z)(1-2\bar{z})},
\]
that is,
\[
x_1^2 + y_1^2 = \frac{(x^2+y^2)\{(x-2)^2+y^2\}}{(1-2x)^2+4y^2},
\]
so that, writing $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$, we have $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$, a correspondence of the two
circumferences. But to the circumference $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ there corresponds not only
the circumference $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$, but another circumference. In fact, writing $x_1^2 + y_1^2 = 1$,
we have
\[
(x^2+y^2)\{(x-2)^2+y^2\} = (1-2x)^2 + 4y^2,
\]
that is,
\[
(x^2+y^2)^2 - 4x(x^2+y^2) + 4x - 1 = 0,
\]
or
\[
(x^2+y^2-1)(x^2+y^2-4x+1) = 0,
\]
and there is thus the other circle
\[
x^2+y^2-4x+1 = 0, \quad \text{or say} \quad (x-2)^2 + y^2 = 3,
\]
viz.~this is a circle, coordinates of centre $(2, 0)$ and radius $= \sqrt{3}$, cutting the circle
$x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ in two real points. Referring to the figures 6 ($xy$) and 7 ($x_1y_1$), and
observing that $x_1 = 0$, $y_1 = 0$, that is, $z_1 = 0$, gives $z = 0$, or $z = 2$, that is, the points
$x = 0$, $y = 0$, and $x = 2$, $y = 0$, we see that to the centre $O$ in figure 7, there
correspond in figure 6 the points $O$, $M$ which are the centres of the two circles.

To any small closed curve, or say any small circle surrounding the point $O$ of
figure 7, there correspond in figure 6 small closed curves surrounding the points
$O$, $M$ respectively; and if in figure 7 the radius of the circle continually increases
and becomes nearly equal to unity, the closed curves of figure 6 continually increase,
changing at the same time their forms, and assume the forms shown by the dotted
lines of figure 6. It thus appears that, to the whole area of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$
of figure 7, there correspond the two lunes $ACE$ and $ABD$ of figure 6; or if we
attend only to the area included within the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ of this figure, then
there corresponds not the whole area of this circle, but only the area of the lune
$ACB$: and thus that the assumed relation $z_1 = \frac{z(z-2)}{1-2z}$ establishes, in fact, an ortho-\allowbreak
morphosis of the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ into the lune $ACB$ which lies inside the circle
$x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ and outside the circle $(x - 2)^2 + y^2 = 3 = 0$. It may be added that, to
the infinite area outside the circle $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ of figure 7, there correspond in
figure 6 first the area of the lens $AB$ common to the two circles, and secondly the
area outside the two circles: we have thus an orthomorphosis of the area outside
the circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$ into these two areas respectively.

A somewhat more elegant example would have been that of the correspondence
\[
z_1 = \frac{z(z-\sqrt{2})}{1-\sqrt{2}\,z};
\]
here, corresponding to the circumference $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$, we have the two equal
circumferences $x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$, and $(x - \sqrt{2})^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$: and to the whole area of the
circle $x_1^2 + y_1^2 - 1 = 0$, there correspond two equal lunes $ACB$ and $ABD$.



\newpage
\section*{922. Note on the Lunar Theory.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}922. Note on the Lunar Theory.}

[From the \textit{Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~LII. (1892), pp.~2--5.]

\bigskip

In the Lunar Theory, in whatever way worked out, the values ultimately obtained
for the coordinates $r$, $v$, $y$ should of course satisfy identically the equations of
motion; and that they do so, is the ultimate verification of the correctness of the
results obtained. It can hardly be hoped for that such a verification will ever be
made for Delaunay's results; and yet it would seem generally that the labour of
such a verification of the results to any extent, while exceeding (and possibly greatly
exceeding) that of obtaining these results by any method employed for that purpose,
ought still to be, so to speak, a labour of the same order. And one can, moreover,
imagine the process of verification having gone on pari passu with the obtainment of
the results.

The verification required is, in fact, that the values of $r$, $v$, $y$, obtained in any
manner, do satisfy identically the equations of motion; these are
\[
\frac{d^2x}{dt^2} + \frac{\mu x}{r^3} = \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x}, \quad
\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + \frac{\mu y}{r^3} = \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y}, \quad
\frac{d^2z}{dt^2} + \frac{\mu z}{r^3} = \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial z},
\]
where $\Omega$ is the disturbing function due to the Sun. I say the verification required is
that these equations are identically satisfied. This does not require the expressions
for $x$, $y$, $z$ to be given each in a single series, but only that the forms be such as
will allow of their being differentiated; and the comparison of the coefficients of the
cosines and sines of the same arguments on the two sides of each equation gives
the relations between the constants and the coefficients, which are in fact the
equations for the determination of these in the method of the variation of the
elements.

\newpage
\textbf{2.} As regards the coordinate $y$, the process of verification was, in fact,
carried out in the memoir \textit{On the Secular Acceleration of the Moon's Mean Motion},
\textit{Philosophical Transactions}, 1859, [516]; the result being that of the two equations
\[
\frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{\partial R}{\partial c^\prime}, \quad \frac{dc^\prime}{dt} + \frac{\partial R}{\partial y} = 0,
\]
the second is satisfied identically by the expressions for $y$ and $c^\prime$, while the first gives
$\frac{\partial R}{\partial c^\prime}$ equal to the calculated value of $\frac{dy}{dt}$.

\textbf{3.} We have $r = a(1 + \rho)$, and we thence obtain $r$ as a function of the time, the
form being in Delaunay's theory $r =$ function of $t + h + \varpi + \zeta$, where $\zeta$ denotes a sum of
cosines with constant coefficients: and thence, by integration, $v = n t + \epsilon +$ function
of the same arguments. The verification of the first two equations would thus
consist in showing that the expressions for $r$ and $v$ satisfy these equations; and the
process would seem to be one which, for any terms taken at pleasure, could be
carried out without too great labour.

\textbf{4.} But it would be interesting to see the process carried out, not for terms
taken at pleasure, but for the whole series as they present themselves in the actual
expressions for the coordinates. I observe that the verification for the coordinates
$r$, $v$ can be made without the explicit expression of $v$ in terms of the time; and that
the verification can be made to depend upon the identical equation $r^2\dot{v} = h$, or say
$\dot{v} = \dfrac{h}{r^2}$. We in fact have $r = \dfrac{a(1-e^2)}{1+e\cos v}$, and thence $r$ as a function of $v$, and we
have in like manner $\rho$, $v$ as functions of $t + h + \varpi + \zeta$; hence $r$, $\dot{v}$, functions of
$t + h + \varpi + \zeta$. The value of $\dot{v}$ obtained in this manner is equal to $\dfrac{h}{r^2}$, and this is
the required verification.

\newpage
\textbf{5.} To complete the verification for the coordinates $r$, $v$, we substitute the
value of $\dot{v}$, and also the value of $r$, in the first equation of motion, say
\[
\ddot{r} - r\dot{v}^2 + \frac{\mu}{r^2} = P;
\]
and the required result is that this equation shall be identically satisfied. The
expression for $r$ is in effect $r = a(1 + \rho)$, where $\rho$ is a sum of cosines; hence $\ddot{r}$ is a sum
of cosines, and the expression for $r\dot{v}^2$ is in like manner a sum of cosines: hence the
left-hand side is a sum of cosines; and the right-hand side $P$, inasmuch as $P$ is a
function of $r$, $v$ given as a sum of cosines, is also a sum of cosines. And if the
verification is to be made, it would consist in the comparison of the two sets of
cosines.

\textbf{6.} The verification should thus ultimately depend on the trigonometrical
identities, and the verification of them would in many cases be an interesting and
not too laborious exercise. As a very simple instance, we may take the following:
in the first approximation we have
\[
r = a(1 - e\cos nt), \quad v = nt + 2e\sin nt,
\]
and thence
\[
\dot{r} = ane\sin nt, \quad \dot{v} = n(1 + 2e\cos nt),
\]
and thus the equations become
\[
ane^2\cos nt - an^2(1 - e\cos nt)(1 + 4e\cos nt) + \frac{\mu}{a^2}(1 + 2e\cos nt) = P;
\]
or, when $e = 0$,
\[
-an^2 + \frac{\mu}{a^2} = 0,
\]
which is right; and, for the terms in $e$,
\[
ae^2n^2\cos nt + 3ane^2n^2\cos nt + \frac{\mu}{a^2}\cdot 2e\cos nt = P;
\]
and thus the equations become
\[
\frac{d^2r}{dt^2} - r\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \frac{\mu}{r^2} = P, \quad
\frac{1}{r}\frac{d}{dt}\left(r^2\frac{dv}{dt}\right) = Q,
\]
where
\[
P = \frac{m^\prime}{a^{\prime 2}}\left(\frac{r}{a^{\prime}} - \frac{a^{\prime 2}r}{r^{\prime 3}}\cos(v - v^\prime)\right), \quad
Q = \frac{m^\prime}{a^{\prime 2}}\left(-\frac{a^{\prime 2}r}{r^{\prime 3}}\sin(v - v^\prime)\right),
\]
and the verification consists in showing that these equations are satisfied identically
by the assumed values of $r$ and $v$.



\newpage
\section*{923. Note on a Hyperdeterminant Identity.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}923. Note on a Hyperdeterminant Identity.}

[From the \textit{Messenger of Mathematics}, vol.~xxi. (1892), pp.~131, 132.]

\bigskip

The following is in effect a well-known theorem; but I am not sure whether
it has been explicitly stated:

If $f$, $g$, $h$ are any functions of $x$, $y$, and we form the matrix
\[
\begin{pmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_1 & g_1 & h_1\\
f_2 & g_2 & h_2
\end{pmatrix},
\]
where the suffixes 1 and 2 denote differentiation in regard to $x$ and $y$ respectively,
then the determinant
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_1 & g_1 & h_1\\
f_2 & g_2 & h_2
\end{vmatrix}
= 0
\]
is satisfied identically when $f$, $g$, $h$ are any linear functions of $x$, $y$; of course it
remains $=0$ when, instead of $x$, $y$, we write any functions whatever of $x$, $y$.

But if $f$, $g$, $h$ are any quadric functions of $x$, $y$, then writing $x$, $y = \dfrac{\xi}{\zeta}$,
$\dfrac{\eta}{\zeta}$, that is, considering $f$, $g$, $h$ as homogeneous functions of $(x, y, z)$, the
condition is
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_1 & g_1 & h_1\\
f_2 & g_2 & h_2
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\]
which (as is obvious, and may be directly verified) is satisfied identically; but here
writing instead of $(x, y, z)$ any functions $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$ of $(x, y, z)$, the condition no longer
remains satisfied identically; it gives in fact a differential equation of the second
order which must be satisfied by $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$, in order that $f$, $g$, $h$, considered as functions
of $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$, may be linear functions of these variables.

\newpage
We may in like manner consider $f$, $g$, $h$ as cubic functions of $(x, y)$; here, regarding
them as homogeneous functions of $(x, y, z)$, the conditions that they may be reducible
to linear functions of $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$ are
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_1 & g_1 & h_1\\
f_2 & g_2 & h_2
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\quad
\begin{vmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_1 & g_1 & h_1\\
f_3 & g_3 & h_3
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\quad
\begin{vmatrix}
f & g & h\\
f_2 & g_2 & h_2\\
f_3 & g_3 & h_3
\end{vmatrix} = 0,
\]
where 1, 2, 3 denote differentiation in regard to $x$, $y$, $z$ respectively. These are all
the same condition, and they are satisfied identically. But if we write for $(x, y, z)$
any functions $(\xi, \eta, \zeta)$ of $(x, y, z)$, then the conditions are differential equations which
must be satisfied by $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$ in order that $f$, $g$, $h$, considered as functions of $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$,
may be linear functions of these variables.



\newpage
\section*{924. On the Non-Existence of a Special Group of Points.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}924. On the Non-Existence of a Special Group of Points.}

[From the \textit{Messenger of Mathematics}, vol.~xxi. (1892), pp.~132, 133.]

\bigskip

It is well known that, taking in a plane any eight points, every cubic through
these passes through a determinate ninth point. It is interesting to show that there
is no system of seven points such that every cubic through these passes through a
determinate eighth point.

Assuming such a system: first, no three of the points can be in a line: for, if
they were, then among the cubics through the seven points we have the line
through the three points and an arbitrary conic through the remaining four points,
and these composite cubics have no common eighth point of intersection.

Secondly, no six of the points can be on a conic: for, if they were, then among
the cubics through the seven points we have the conic through the six points and
an arbitrary line through the remaining point, and these composite cubics have no
common eighth point of intersection.

Taking now the points to be $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$, $6$, $7$; among the cubics through
these, we have the composite cubics $(A, P)$, $(B, Q)$, $(C, R)$, where $A$, $B$, $C$ are the
lines $67$, $75$, $56$, and $P$, $Q$, $R$ the conics $12345$, $12346$, $12347$ respectively; by what
precedes, the points $5$, $6$, $7$ do not lie on a line, and the points $(6, 7)$, $(7, 5)$ and
$(5, 6)$ neither of them lie on the conics $P$, $Q$, $R$ respectively.

The common eighth point of intersection, if it exists, must be
\[
(A, B, C);\quad (A, Q, R),\ (B, R, P),\ (C, P, Q);\quad
(P, B, C),\ (Q, C, A),\ (R, A, B);\quad \text{or}\ (P, Q, R).
\]
There is no point $(A, B, C)$.

There is no point $(A, Q, R)$: for $Q$, $R$ intersect only in the points $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$,
no one of which lies on $A$; and similarly, there is no point $(B, R, P)$ or $(C, P, Q)$.

$B$, $C$ intersect in $5$, which is a point on $P$; and thus $5$ is the only point
$(P, B, C)$. Similarly, $6$ is the only point $(Q, C, A)$ and $7$ is the only point $(R, A, B)$.

$P$, $Q$ intersect in $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$, which are each of them on $R$; hence $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$
are the only points $(P, Q, R)$.

Hence the points $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$, $6$, $7$ present themselves each once, and only
once, among the intersections of the three cubics, and there is no common eighth point
of intersection.



\newpage
\section*{925. On Waring's Formula for the Sum of the $m$th Powers of the Roots of an Equation.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}925. On Waring's Formula for the Sum of the $m$th Powers of the Roots of an Equation.}

[From the \textit{Messenger of Mathematics}, vol.~xxi. (1892), pp.~133--137.]

\bigskip

The formula in question, Prob.~I.~of Waring's \textit{Meditationes Algebraic\ae}, Cambridge,
1782, making therein a slight change of notation, is as follows: viz. the equation being
\[
x^n - bx^{n-1} + cx^{n-2} - dx^{n-3} + \ldots = 0,
\]
then we have
\begin{align*}
(-)^{m-1} s_m &= mb^m - m\,c\\
&\quad - m\,b^{m-2}c + m(m-3)\,bd\\
&\quad + m\,b^{m-3}d - m(m-4)\,ce - m(m-4)\,bd + \tfrac{1}{2}m(m-4)(m-5)\,c^2d\\
&\quad + m\,b^{m-4}e + m(m-5)\,bf + m(m-5)\,ce\\
&\quad - m(m-5)(m-6)\,cd^2 - m(m-5)(m-6)\,be\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{6}m(m-5)(m-6)(m-7)\,c^3 - m\,g\\
&\quad - m(m-6)\,ch - m(m-6)\,dg - m(m-6)\,bf\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{2}m(m-6)(m-7)\,cd^2 - m(m-6)\,eg\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{2}m(m-6)(m-7)\,d^2f - \tfrac{1}{2}m(m-6)(m-7)\,ce^2\\
&\quad - \tfrac{1}{6}m(m-6)(m-7)(m-8)\,c^2e + \tfrac{1}{2}m(m-6)(m-7)\,d^3\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{24}m(m-6)(m-7)(m-8)(m-9)\,c^4 + \&\text{c.},
\end{align*}
where, reckoning the weights of $b$, $c$, $d$, $e$, \ldots\ as $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$, \ldots, respectively, the several
terms are all the terms of the weight $m$, or (what is the same thing) in the
coefficient of $b^{m-\theta}$ we have all the combinations of $c$, $d$, $e$, \ldots\ (or say all the
non-unitary combinations) of the weight $\theta$, and where the numerical coefficient of
\[
b^{m-\theta} c^d d^e e^f \ldots \quad (d + e + f + \ldots = \theta),
\]
is
\[
\frac{m \cdot m - (\theta + 1) \cdot m - (\theta + 2) \ldots m - (\theta - 1)}{c!\,d!\,e!\,f!\ldots}.
\]
Thus for the term $b^{m-8}c^2e^4$, $\theta = 8$; $c$, $d$, $e = 2$, $0$, $4$, $1$ respectively (the other exponents
each vanishing), and the coefficient is
\[
\frac{m \cdot m-6 \cdot m-7}{1 \cdot 2} = -\tfrac{1}{2}m(m-6)(m-7),
\]
as above; and so in other cases.

For the MacMahon form
\[
1 + bx + \frac{c}{1 \cdot 2}x^2 + \frac{d}{1 \cdot 2 \cdot 3}x^3 + \ldots = (1 - \alpha x)(1 - \beta x)(1 - \gamma x)\ldots,
\]
or say
\[
1 + bx + \frac{c}{1 \cdot 2}x^2 + \ldots = (1 - \alpha x)(1 - \beta x)\ldots,
\]
we must for $b$, $c$, $d$, \ldots\ write $b$, $\frac{1}{1 \cdot 2}c$, $\frac{1}{1 \cdot 2 \cdot 3}d$, \ldots\ respectively: we thus have
\begin{align*}
(-)^m \Pi(m-1) \cdot S_m &= \Pi(m-1) \cdot b^m\\
&\quad - \Pi(m-2) \cdot b^{m-2}c\\
&\quad + \Pi(m-3) \cdot b^{m-3}d\\
&\quad + \Pi(m-3) \cdot b^{m-3}cd\\
&\quad - \Pi(m-4) \cdot b^{m-4}e - \Pi(m-4) \cdot b^{m-4}c^2\\
&\quad + \&\text{c.},
\end{align*}
or say
\begin{multline*}
(-)^m \Pi(m-1) S_m = \Pi(m-1)b^m - \Pi(m-2)b^{m-2}c + \Pi(m-3)b^{m-3}d\\
+ \Pi(m-3)b^{m-3}cd - \Pi(m-4)b^{m-4}e - \Pi(m-4)b^{m-4}c^2 + \&\text{c.},
\end{multline*}
the numerical coefficient of
\[
b^{m-\theta} c^d d^e e^f \ldots \quad (d + e + f + \ldots = \theta)
\]
being
\[
\frac{(c + e + g + \ldots)m \cdot m - (\theta - \delta + 1) \cdot m - (\theta - \delta + 2) \ldots m - (\delta - 1)}{c!\,d!\,e!\ldots (1 \cdot 2)^c (1 \cdot 2 \cdot 3)^d (1 \cdot 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 4)^e \ldots}.
\]

\newpage
It is convenient to write down the literal terms in alphabetical order (AO),
calculating and affixing to each term the proper numerical coefficient; thus taking
\[
1 + bx + cx^2 + dx^3 + \ldots = (1 - \alpha x)(1 - \beta x)(1 - \gamma x)\ldots,
\]
we find
\begin{align*}
-120b^6 &= g \cdot 1\\
&\quad + f \cdot -6\\
&\quad + ce \cdot -15\\
&\quad + d^2 \cdot -10\\
&\quad + b^2e \cdot +30\\
&\quad + bcd \cdot +120\\
&\quad + c^3 \cdot +30\\
&\quad + b^3d \cdot -120\\
&\quad + b^2c^2 \cdot -270\\
&\quad + b^4c \cdot +360\\
&\quad + b^6 \cdot -120\\
&\quad \pm 541,
\end{align*}
this expression, as representing the value of the non-unitary function $S_6$, being in
fact a seminvariant.

It is to be remarked that the foregoing expression for the sum of the $m$th
powers of the roots of the equation
\[
x^n - bx^{n-1} + cx^{n-2} - \ldots = 0
\]
is, in fact, the series for $s_m$ continued so far only as the exponent of $b$ is not
negative: see as to this Note~XI.~of Lagrange's \textit{Equations Num\'eriques}. For the $a$
posteriori verification, observe that we have
\[
s_m = \alpha^m + \beta^m + \gamma^m + \ldots,
\]
or writing for a moment $u = -b$, say this is
\[
s_m = (-u)^m + (-u - c)^m + (-u - d)^m + \ldots,
\]
where
\[
f(x) = x^n - bx^{n-1} + cx^{n-2} - dx^{n-3} + \&\text{c.} = 0.
\]
Hence, by Lagrange's theorem,
\[
s_m = \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int \frac{x^m f^\prime(x)}{f(x)} dx,
\]
and the residue at infinity gives the required expression.



\newpage
\section*{926. Corrected Seminvariant Tables for the Weights 11 and 12.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\numberline{}926. Corrected Seminvariant Tables for the Weights 11 and 12.}

[From the \textit{Proceedings of the Royal Society}, vol.~L. (1891), pp.~431--437.]

\bigskip

The annexed Tables, extending those for the weights 1 to 10 given in my
``Memoir on Seminvariants,'' [875], require a correction. In the Tables, weight~10,
last two columns but one, instead of the columns $cdf - b^4d^2$, $ce^3 - c^5$, we ought to have
$ce^3 - c^5$, $cdf - b^4d^2$. The complete list up to the weight 12 is
\begin{align*}
w &= 1 && b\\
w &= 2 && c\\
w &= 3 && d,\ b^2\\
w &= 4 && e,\ bc\\
w &= 5 && f,\ bd,\ c^2\\
w &= 6 && g,\ be,\ cd,\ b^2c\\
w &= 7 && h,\ bf,\ ce,\ d^2,\ b^2d,\ bc^2\\
w &= 8 && i,\ bg,\ cf,\ de,\ b^2e,\ bcd,\ c^3,\ b^3c\\
w &= 9 && j,\ bh,\ cg,\ df,\ e^2,\ b^2f,\ bce,\ cd^2,\ bc^2d,\ b^3d,\ b^2c^2\\
w &= 10 && k,\ bi,\ ch,\ dg,\ ef,\ b^2g,\ bcf,\ bde,\ c^2d,\ b^3e,\\
&&& b^2cd,\ bc^3,\ b^4c;\quad cd^2f,\ ce^3 - c^5
\end{align*}
